Each layer does one job and serves the layer above it. Techs use it to say where a problem lives, like \"that is a Layer 1 issue.\" Count from the bottom: 1 Physical, 2 Data Link, 3 Network, 4 Transport, 5 Session, 6 Presentation, 7 Application.","hook":"Please Do Not Throw Sausage Pizza Away: layers 1 to 7."},{"title":"Layers 1 and 2","body":"Layer 1, Physical, moves raw bits as electrical, light or radio signals. Cables, connectors, hubs, repeaters and transceivers live here. Layer 2, Data Link, moves frames between devices on the same local network using MAC addresses. Switches work here. Layer 2 also checks each frame for errors with a frame check sequence."},{"title":"Layer 3 Network","body":"Layer 3 moves packets between different networks. It uses logical addresses, such as IPv4 and IPv6 addresses. Routers make forwarding decisions here by reading the destination IP address. ICMP, the protocol behind ping, also works at Layer 3. A Layer 3 switch can route between VLANs. If two subnets cannot talk, think Layer 3."},{"title":"Layer 4 Transport","body":"Layer 4 moves data between applications on two hosts. TCP is connection-oriented. It opens with a three-way handshake and uses sequence numbers and acknowledgments, so lost data is resent. UDP is connectionless, with no handshake and less overhead. Port numbers live here and identify the service, such as 443 for HTTPS. TCP units are segments; UDP units are datagrams."},{"title":"Layers 5, 6 and 7","body":"Layer 5, Session, sets up, manages and ends sessions between applications. Layer 6, Presentation, formats and translates data, such as character encoding, compression and encryption. Layer 7, Application, is where network services meet the software, through protocols such as HTTP, DNS and SMTP. Layer 7 is the protocol the app uses, not the app itself."},{"title":"Encapsulation and Data Units","body":"As data moves down the stack, each layer adds its own header. This is encapsulation. ESP encrypts."},{"title":"Unicast to Anycast","body":"Unicast is one sender to one receiver. Broadcast is one sender to every host on the local network, such as an ARP request or a DHCP discover. Anycast gives several servers one address, and routing delivers to the nearest. BNC is a twist-lock coax connector."},{"title":"Single-Mode Versus Multimode","body":"Single-mode fiber has a tiny core, about 9 microns, and uses lasers. That modal dispersion limits it to shorter runs, typically a few hundred meters at 10 Gbps. The optics and fiber type must match."},{"title":"Transceivers and DAC","body":"A transceiver plugs into a switch port and converts signals for the media. It is easy to manage, but the center is a single point of failure. In a full mesh, every node connects to every other node, giving high redundancy at high cost. A full mesh of n nodes needs n(n-1)/2 links. A hybrid mixes topologies."},{"title":"Point to Point Links","body":"A point to point link connects exactly two endpoints. Examples are two routers on a leased line or a wireless bridge between two buildings. Nothing else shares the link, so it is simple to plan and troubleshoot. A hub and spoke WAN is built from many point to point links that all meet at one central site, such as the main campus."},{"title":"Three-Tier Hierarchical Model","body":"The access layer connects end devices, such as PCs, phones, printers and APs. The distribution layer aggregates access switches and applies policy, such as routing between VLANs and filtering. The core layer is a fast, redundant backbone that moves traffic between distribution blocks. Keep the core simple so it can forward quickly.","hook":"Access plugs in. Distribution decides. Core carries."},{"title":"The Collapsed Core Design","body":"A collapsed core merges the core and distribution layers into one layer. Smaller campuses use it to save money and equipment while keeping a separate access layer. This gives a two-tier design. If the network grows, the team can later split out a dedicated core layer."},{"title":"Spine and Leaf","body":"Spine and leaf is a data center design. Every leaf switch connects to every spine switch, and servers connect to the leaves. In the classic design, leaves do not link to other leaves, and spines do not link to other spines. Any server reaches any other in the same number of hops, so latency is predictable. Add spines for bandwidth and leaves for ports."},{"title":"North-South and East-West","body":"North-south traffic enters or leaves the data center, such as a student at home loading a campus web page. Private addresses are not routed on the internet, so traffic leaving the campus uses NAT to get a public address.","hook":"Ten, 172.16 through 31, and 192.168."},{"title":"APIPA and Loopback","body":"When a host set for DHCP gets no answer, it can give itself a link-local address from 169.254.0.0/16. Windows calls this APIPA. The address works only on the local link, and routers never forward it. Seeing 169.254 usually means a DHCP problem. Loopback is 127.0.0.0/8. Pinging 127.0.0.1, or localhost, tests the host's own TCP/IP stack."},{"title":"Classful Addressing Basics","body":"Classes set old default boundaries by the first octet. Class A is 1 to 126, with a /8 default mask. Class B is 128 to 191, /16. Class C is 192 to 223, /24. Class D, 224 to 239, is multicast. Class E, 240 to 255, is reserved for experimental use. 127 is reserved for loopback. Modern networks use CIDR, but the exam still tests classes."},{"title":"CIDR and Host Counts","body":"CIDR writes the mask as a prefix length, such as /24 for 255.255.255.0. Host bits equal 32 minus the prefix. Usable hosts equal 2 raised to the host bits, minus 2 for the network and broadcast addresses. A /26 has 6 host bits, so 64 addresses and 62 usable hosts. CIDR also lets four /24 networks be summarized as one /22.","hook":"Usable hosts = 2 to the host bits, minus 2."},{"title":"Network and Broadcast Math","body":"Find the block size: 256 minus the mask value in the octet that is changing. A /27 mask is 255.255.255.224, so the block size is 32. Subnets start at 0, 32, 64, 96 and so on. For 10.1.1.77/27, the network is 10.1.1.64 and the broadcast is 10.1.1.95. Usable hosts run from .65 to .94.","hook":"Block size = 256 minus the mask octet."},{"title":"VLSM in Practice","body":"Variable length subnet masking lets each subnet use a different prefix length, so fewer addresses are wasted. NIST SP 800-207 describes this model.","hook":"Never trust, always verify."},{"title":"SASE and SSE","body":"Secure access service edge, or SASE, delivers networking and security together from the cloud. It uses no CPU or bandwidth for updates, but it never adapts when a link fails. A default route (0.0.0.0/0) is the gateway of last resort for traffic with no better match. Dynamic routing protocols let routers share routes and reroute around failures on their own, which suits larger or changing networks.","hook":"Static stays put. Dynamic adapts."},{"title":"OSPF: link-state routing","body":"OSPF is an open-standard interior routing protocol. It is link-state: each router floods link-state advertisements (LSAs), builds a full map of its area, and runs the SPF (Dijkstra) algorithm to find the lowest-cost path. Cost is based on interface bandwidth. Large networks are split into areas, and every area must connect to backbone area 0.","hook":"OSPF draws the whole map, then picks the cheapest path."},{"title":"EIGRP and BGP","body":"EIGRP is a Cisco-developed interior protocol from the distance-vector family, often called advanced distance vector. It uses the DUAL algorithm, sends partial updates only when something changes, and by default builds its metric from bandwidth and delay. BGP is the exterior protocol of the internet. It is a path-vector protocol that routes between autonomous systems over TCP port 179.","hook":"BGP links autonomous systems. Think ISPs and the internet."},{"title":"How a router picks a route","body":"First, the longest matching prefix wins, so a /26 beats a /24 for an address inside both. If different sources offer the same prefix, the lowest administrative distance (AD) wins. Cisco defaults: connected 0, static 1, eBGP 20, internal EIGRP 90, OSPF 110, RIP 120, iBGP 200. Within one protocol, the lowest metric wins.","hook":"Longest prefix, then lowest AD, then lowest metric."},{"title":"NAT and PAT","body":"Network address translation (NAT) rewrites IP addresses as packets cross a router, usually private inside addresses to public ones. Static NAT maps one inside address to one public address, which lets outside users reach an inside server. Port address translation (PAT), also called NAT overload, rewrites port numbers too, so many inside hosts can share one public IP.","hook":"PAT: many hosts, one public IP, told apart by port."},{"title":"FHRP, VIP and subinterfaces","body":"A First Hop Redundancy Protocol (FHRP) lets two or more routers share a virtual IP (VIP) and virtual MAC. Hosts use the VIP as their default gateway. If the active router fails, a backup takes over the same address. HSRP is a Cisco FHRP, and VRRP is the open IETF standard. Subinterfaces split one physical port into logical ports, one per VLAN, using 802.1Q tags.","hook":"One cable, many VLANs: router on a stick uses subinterfaces."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Help desk","report":"A new technician is reading the core router's routing table and asks how much the router trusts routes it learns from OSPF.","question":"What is the default Cisco administrative distance for OSPF?","options":["20","90","110","120"],"answer":2,"why":"Cisco's default AD for OSPF is 110. 90 is internal EIGRP, 120 is RIP and 20 is external BGP. A lower AD means a more trusted source."},{"place":"Data center","report":"The core router has a static route to 10.20.0.0/16 and an EIGRP route to 10.20.30.0/24. Users are trying to reach a server at 10.20.30.5.","question":"Which route does the router use for traffic to 10.20.30.5?","options":["Neither, so it uses the default route","The static route, because its AD is lower","Both routes, load balanced equally","The EIGRP route, because its prefix is longer"],"answer":3,"why":"Longest prefix match comes first, and the /24 is more specific than the /16. AD only breaks ties between routes to the same prefix, so the static route's lower AD does not matter here."},{"place":"IT office","report":"A manager is writing a change request to add OSPF in the residence halls and wants one accurate sentence on how OSPF works.","question":"Which statement best describes OSPF?","options":["Path-vector protocol that routes between autonomous systems","Link-state protocol that runs SPF on a topology map","Cisco-developed protocol using DUAL and partial updates","Manual method that needs updates after every change"],"answer":1,"why":"OSPF is an open link-state protocol. Routers flood LSAs, build a map and run SPF. Path-vector routing between autonomous systems describes BGP, and DUAL describes EIGRP."},{"place":"Admissions office","report":"The office has 40 PCs with private 10.x addresses. OSPF and EIGRP are interior protocols used inside one organization."},{"place":"Data center","report":"The edge router learns its default route from OSPF. At 130 it stays out of the table until the OSPF route (AD 110) is lost. AD 1 would override OSPF right away."}]},{"id":"2.2","title":"Given a scenario, configure switching technologies and features","lessons":[{"title":"VLANs, database and SVIs","body":"A VLAN splits one switch into separate broadcast domains, so traffic between VLANs must be routed. On Cisco switches, VLANs 1 to 1005 are stored in the VLAN database file (vlan.dat) in flash, and VLAN 1 is the default that cannot be deleted. A switch virtual interface (SVI) gives a VLAN an IP address, so a Layer 3 switch can route between VLANs.","hook":"SVI: the VLAN's gateway, living inside the switch."},{"title":"Trunks, 802.1Q, native VLAN","body":"An access port carries one VLAN. A trunk carries many VLANs between switches, routers or APs. IEEE 802.1Q inserts a 4-byte tag that holds a 12-bit VLAN ID, and usable IDs run up to 4094. Frames on the native VLAN cross the trunk untagged. Both ends must use the same native VLAN, or untagged traffic lands in the wrong VLAN.","hook":"Native VLAN means no tag. Make it match on both ends."},{"title":"Voice VLANs for IP phones","body":"Many desks have a PC plugged into an IP phone, which plugs into one switch port. A voice VLAN lets that access port carry two VLANs. The PC's data stays untagged in the access (data) VLAN. The phone tags its voice traffic into the voice VLAN, so it can get QoS priority. The switch tells the phone which voice VLAN to use with CDP or LLDP-MED."},{"title":"Link aggregation with LACP","body":"Link aggregation bundles several physical links into one logical link for more bandwidth and redundancy. Cisco calls it EtherChannel. LACP is the IEEE negotiation protocol (802.3ad, now in 802.1AX). Active mode starts negotiation and passive mode only answers, so passive on both ends never forms a bundle. Members must match speed, duplex and VLAN settings. Spanning tree treats the bundle as one link.","hook":"Passive plus passive equals nothing. One side must be active."},{"title":"Spanning Tree Protocol","body":"Redundant switch links create loops, where broadcasts circle forever and MAC tables keep changing. Spanning Tree (IEEE 802.1D) blocks the extra paths. Switches trade BPDUs and elect a root bridge: the lowest bridge ID wins, comparing priority first, then MAC address. Default priority is 32768. Classic ports move through blocking, listening, learning and forwarding. Rapid STP (802.1w) converges much faster.","hook":"Lowest bridge ID is root. Lower the priority to pick the root."},{"title":"Speed, duplex, MTU, jumbo","body":"Set speed and duplex to auto on both ends, or hard-code both ends the same. A duplex mismatch causes slow transfers, late collisions and CRC errors. The standard Ethernet MTU is 1500 bytes. Jumbo frames are larger, often about 9000 bytes, and help storage and backup traffic. Jumbo sizes are not an IEEE standard, so every device in the path must support them.","hook":"Duplex mismatch: slow link plus late collisions."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Help desk","report":"A trainee is reading a packet capture from a trunk link and asks how much the VLAN tag adds to each Ethernet frame.","question":"How many bytes does an 802.1Q tag add to a frame?","options":["2 bytes","4 bytes","8 bytes","12 bytes"],"answer":1,"why":"802.1Q inserts a 4-byte tag between the source MAC and the type field. The 12-bit VLAN ID inside the tag is easy to confuse with its size, but 12 is bits, not bytes."},{"place":"Dorm C","report":"Two switches share a trunk. The VLAN database only stores which VLANs exist. It does not route."}]},{"id":"2.3","title":"Given a scenario, select and configure wireless devices and technologies","lessons":[{"title":"2.4, 5 and 6 GHz bands","body":"2.4 GHz reaches farthest and passes through walls best, but microwaves and Bluetooth crowd it and it has few channels. 5 GHz offers many more channels and higher speeds with shorter range. 6 GHz, opened to Wi-Fi with Wi-Fi 6E, adds the most clean spectrum but has the shortest range. Band steering nudges dual-band clients off 2.4 GHz onto a higher band.","hook":"Lower frequency, longer reach. Higher frequency, more room."},{"title":"Channels and channel width","body":"Neighboring APs should use non-overlapping channels. In the US 2.4 GHz band, only channels 1, 6 and 11 do not overlap, so keep 20 MHz width there. In 5 and 6 GHz, channels can be bonded to 40, 80 or 160 MHz. Wider channels give one client more speed but leave fewer separate channels, which hurts crowded areas.","hook":"2.4 GHz: 1, 6, 11. Nothing else."},{"title":"Regulations and 802.11h","body":"Each country sets which channels and power levels are legal, so APs need the correct country setting. IEEE 802.11h added two tools for 5 GHz: Dynamic Frequency Selection (DFS) and Transmit Power Control (TPC). DFS channels are shared with radar. When an AP detects radar, it must leave the channel and stay off it for at least 30 minutes.","hook":"DFS dodges radar. TPC turns the power down."},{"title":"SSIDs and network types","body":"The SSID is the network name users see. A BSSID is the MAC address that identifies one AP radio's cell. An ESSID is one SSID shared by many APs so users can roam. Infrastructure mode sends clients through an AP. Ad hoc connects devices directly with no AP. Mesh APs relay traffic to each other wirelessly. Point to point joins two sites, often with directional antennas."},{"title":"WPA2, WPA3, PSK, Enterprise","body":"WPA2 encrypts with AES-CCMP. WPA3-Personal replaces the pre-shared key handshake with SAE, which resists offline password guessing. Every WPA3 connection must use protected management frames. Personal mode (PSK or SAE) uses one shared passphrase. Enterprise mode uses 802.1X with a RADIUS server, so each user signs in with their own credentials. The 6 GHz band allows only WPA3 or Enhanced Open, never WPA2.","hook":"Personal: one shared password. Enterprise: each user logs in."},{"title":"Guests, antennas and AP types","body":"Guest networks use a separate SSID and VLAN with internet-only access. A captive portal redirects a guest's browser to a page to sign in or accept terms first. Omnidirectional antennas cover all around, like a ceiling AP. Directional antennas focus signal one way, for hallways or links between buildings. Autonomous APs are configured one by one. Lightweight APs need a wireless LAN controller.","hook":"Lightweight APs lean on the controller."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Help desk","report":"A technician is planning 2.4 GHz channels for three APs in a small US office and wants none of them to overlap.","question":"Which 2.4 GHz channels do not overlap in the US?","options":["1, 6 and 11","1, 5 and 9","36, 40 and 44","1, 7 and 13"],"answer":0,"why":"With 20 MHz channels in the US 2.4 GHz band, only 1, 6 and 11 avoid overlap. Autonomous APs are configured one at a time and run on their own."}]},{"id":"2.4","title":"Explain important factors of physical installations","lessons":[{"title":"MDF and IDF","body":"The main distribution frame (MDF) is the main wiring room. It usually holds the building's cable entrance and the core switches or routers. Intermediate distribution frames (IDFs) are smaller closets, often one per floor, that serve nearby work areas. Each IDF connects back to the MDF with backbone cabling, usually fiber. Horizontal cable runs from a closet to desks stay within 90 meters.","hook":"MDF is the main hub. IDFs are the floor closets."},{"title":"Racks, rack units, airflow","body":"Network gear mounts in 19-inch racks measured in rack units (U). One U is 1.75 inches, and full racks are often 42U. Rack fronts face cold aisles, where cool air enters. Backs face hot aisles, where hot air leaves. Port-side exhaust gear takes air in at the fan and power supply end and blows it out the port side. Port-side intake is the reverse. Fans and power supplies must match.","hook":"The name tells you what the port side does with air."},{"title":"Patch panels and locks","body":"A patch panel terminates horizontal copper runs at the rack. Short patch cords then connect each port to a switch, so moves and changes never disturb the permanent cable. A fiber distribution panel does the same job for fiber, protecting splices and connectors and managing bend radius. Lockable racks, cabinets and enclosures keep unauthorized people away from gear, especially in shared rooms."},{"title":"Choosing a UPS type","body":"An uninterruptible power supply (UPS) uses batteries to ride through outages. A standby UPS switches to battery only when power fails. A line-interactive UPS also corrects high or low voltage without using the battery. An online double-conversion UPS always runs the load through its inverter, so there is zero transfer time. Size a UPS for the load's watts and the runtime you need.","hook":"Online double-conversion: zero transfer time, best protection."},{"title":"PDUs, power load, voltage","body":"A power distribution unit (PDU) spreads power to the devices in a rack. It has no battery. Metered PDUs report load, and switched PDUs can also power-cycle outlets remotely. Add up device watts to plan load. North American rack PDUs are rated at 80 percent of the circuit, so 30 A allows 24 A. Higher voltage, like 208 V, draws fewer amps for the same watts.","hook":"Plan to 80 percent of the breaker, never 100."},{"title":"Temperature, humidity, fire","body":"Measure temperature at the equipment air inlets. ASHRAE recommends 18 to 27 C (about 64 to 81 F). Air that is too dry raises static discharge risk, and air that is too humid risks condensation and corrosion. For fire, keep water off live gear. Clean agent gas systems leave no residue. Pre-action sprinklers keep their pipes dry until detectors confirm a fire."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Data center","report":"A technician is checking whether a new 2U firewall and a 1U switch will fit in the 3U of open space left in a rack.","question":"How tall is one rack unit (1U)?","options":["1 inch","1.75 inches","2.5 inches","19 inches"],"answer":1,"why":"One rack unit is 1.75 inches, so the 2U and 1U devices need exactly 3U, or 5.25 inches. 19 inches is the standard rack width, not the height of one unit."},{"place":"Data center","report":"A new top-of-rack switch will be mounted with its ports facing the hot aisle. The MIB is the set of objects a device can report, and each object has a numeric OID.","hook":"Poll on 161. Trap on 162."},{"title":"SNMP versions and security","body":"SNMPv2c uses community strings, which act like shared passwords. They are sent in clear text, so anyone capturing traffic can read them. SNMPv3 adds per-user authentication, integrity checks, and optional encryption. Its security levels are noAuthNoPriv, authNoPriv, and authPriv. Use authPriv when you need both authentication and privacy. Never leave well-known strings like public in use.","hook":"v3 is the only version with encryption."},{"title":"Flow data and packet capture","body":"Flow data, such as NetFlow or IPFIX, summarizes conversations: source and destination addresses, ports, protocol, and byte counts. Use packet capture to see exactly what went wrong.","hook":"Flow is the phone bill. Capture is the recording."},{"title":"Seeing traffic with port mirroring","body":"A switch forwards unicast frames only to the port where the destination lives. A laptop on another port sees only broadcasts and flooded traffic. Port mirroring, called SPAN on Cisco switches, copies traffic from chosen ports or VLANs to a monitor port. Plug your analyzer or sensor into that port to capture the traffic you need."},{"title":"Baselines, logs, and SIEM","body":"A baseline records normal behavior, like usual bandwidth or CPU at peak hours. If the RPO is 4 hours, backups or replication must happen at least every 4 hours. A shorter RPO needs more frequent backups or real-time replication, which costs more. RPO is about data, not about how long the outage lasts.","hook":"RPO looks back at data. RTO looks ahead to uptime."},{"title":"RTO: how long to recover","body":"The recovery time objective (RTO) is the longest a system can stay down before the impact is unacceptable. If the RTO is 2 hours, the recovery plan must have the service running within 2 hours. RTO drives choices like the DR site type and the failover design. RTO is about time, not about how much data is lost."},{"title":"MTTR and MTBF","body":"Mean time to repair (MTTR) is the average time it takes to fix a failed component and restore service. Lower is better. Mean time between failures (MTBF) is the average run time between failures of a repairable device. Higher is better. Use MTBF to judge reliability and MTTR to judge how fast your team recovers.","hook":"Between failures: want it big. To repair: want it small."},{"title":"Cold, warm, and hot sites","body":"A cold site has space, power, cooling, and network links, but little or no equipment. It is cheapest and slowest to bring online. A warm site has some or all of the hardware but still needs setup and data restored. A hot site is fully equipped and staffed, ready to take over quickly. It costs the most.","hook":"Hotter site, faster recovery, bigger bill."},{"title":"Active-active vs active-passive","body":"High availability removes single points of failure. In active-active, two or more systems carry traffic at the same time and share the load. If one fails, the rest keep going, so they need room for the full load. In active-passive, one system works while a standby waits and takes over on failure. Active-passive is simpler to run."},{"title":"Testing the DR plan","body":"A plan you never test may fail when you need it. A tabletop exercise is discussion-based: the team talks through a scenario and their roles, and no systems are touched. Validation tests actually run recovery steps, such as restoring backups or failing over to the DR site, to prove the plan meets its RPO and RTO.","hook":"Tabletop talks. Validation does."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Help desk","report":"Leadership says the student portal may lose no more than 15 minutes of data in a disaster.","question":"Which metric did leadership just set?","options":["RPO","RTO","MTBF","MTTR"],"answer":0,"why":"Acceptable data loss measured in time is the RPO. RTO is a target the business sets, not the measured repair time."}]},{"id":"3.4","title":"Given a scenario, implement IPv4 and IPv6 network services","lessons":[{"title":"DHCP DORA and relay","body":"DHCP hands out IP settings automatically. The client broadcasts a Discover, servers send an Offer, the client sends a Request, and the server confirms with an Ack. Clients use UDP 68 and servers use UDP 67. Routers do not forward broadcasts, so a relay agent, set with ip helper-address on Cisco, forwards them to a server on another subnet.","hook":"DORA: Discover, Offer, Request, Ack."},{"title":"Scopes, exclusions, reservations, options","body":"A scope is the address range a DHCP server hands out on one subnet. Exclusions remove addresses from the scope, like static server addresses. A reservation always gives one client the same address, usually matched by its MAC. Lease time sets how long a client keeps an address. By default, renewal starts at half the lease. Options add settings: 3 is the default gateway, 6 is DNS servers."},{"title":"IPv6 SLAAC and router advertisements","body":"Stateless address autoconfiguration (SLAAC) lets an IPv6 host build its own address with no server. It first makes an fe80:: link-local address. It sends a Router Solicitation, and routers reply with a Router Advertisement holding a /64 prefix. The host adds its own 64-bit interface ID and runs Duplicate Address Detection. The RA's M and O flags point hosts to DHCPv6.","hook":"SLAAC: the router gives the prefix, the host builds the rest."},{"title":"DNS records and zones","body":"An A record maps a name to an IPv4 address, and AAAA maps it to IPv6. A CNAME is an alias that points to another name. MX names the mail server, and the lower preference value wins. TXT holds text, such as SPF mail rules. NS lists the zone's name servers. Forward zones map names to addresses. Reverse zones hold PTR records that map addresses back to names.","hook":"Quad A is four times the bits."},{"title":"DNS servers, security, hosts file","body":"An authoritative server holds the zone and answers with authority. An answer from a cache is non-authoritative. The primary holds the editable copy, and secondaries keep read-only copies through zone transfers. A recursive resolver chases answers for clients. DNSSEC signs records but does not encrypt. DoT (TCP 853) and DoH (HTTPS, usually 443) encrypt queries. Windows checks its hosts file before asking DNS.","hook":"DNSSEC proves it. DoT and DoH hide it."},{"title":"Time protocols: NTP, PTP, NTS","body":"Accurate time matters for logs, certificates, and Kerberos logins. NTP syncs clocks over UDP 123. Stratum 1 servers connect to a reference clock like GPS, each level below adds one, and stratum 16 means unsynchronized. NTS adds cryptographic authentication to NTP, with a TLS 1.3 key exchange on TCP 4460. PTP (IEEE 1588) uses hardware timestamps for much finer accuracy."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Help desk","report":"A new tech asks what order of messages a laptop uses when it gets an address from DHCP.","question":"Which order is correct?","options":["Discover, Offer, Request, Ack","Request, Offer, Discover, Ack","Discover, Request, Offer, Ack","Offer, Discover, Ack, Request"],"answer":0,"why":"DHCP follows DORA: Discover, Offer, Request, Ack. A gateway at each end, usually a router or firewall, builds the encrypted tunnel, often with IPsec. Users at either site need no VPN software, because their traffic is routed into the tunnel for them. The tunnel normally stays up all the time.","hook":"Site-to-site: the gateways do the work."},{"title":"Client-to-site and clientless VPNs","body":"A client-to-site, or remote access, VPN connects one user's device to the campus network. The user runs VPN client software and signs in. A clientless VPN needs only a web browser. The user signs in to an HTTPS portal that gives access to chosen web apps and file shares. Clientless is easy for borrowed devices but reaches fewer resources."},{"title":"Split vs full tunnel","body":"In a full tunnel, all of a remote device's traffic goes through the VPN, including internet browsing. Campus can inspect and filter it, but it uses more VPN bandwidth. In a split tunnel, only campus-bound traffic uses the VPN, and internet traffic goes straight out the local connection. Split tunnel saves bandwidth, but campus filters never see that traffic.","hook":"Full tunnel: everything goes home. Split: only work goes home."},{"title":"SSH, GUI, API, console","body":"SSH gives an encrypted command line on TCP 22. Use it instead of Telnet, which sends everything, passwords included, in clear text. A GUI is a web or app interface, ideally over HTTPS. An API lets scripts and tools manage devices in software, which is great for automation. A console connection plugs a cable straight into the device and works with no network settings."},{"title":"Jump boxes for admin access","body":"A jump box, or jump host, is a hardened server that admins connect to first, then use to reach devices in a protected zone. Management interfaces accept connections only from the jump box. That gives one controlled, logged entry point. Keep it patched and protect it with strong authentication, because it holds powerful access."},{"title":"In-band vs out-of-band management","body":"In-band management uses the same network that carries user traffic. It is cheap and easy, but if that network breaks, you lose your management access too. Out-of-band management uses a separate path, such as a dedicated management network or console servers. It keeps working during outages, so you can fix the device that broke the network.","hook":"Out-of-band is your back door when the front door fails."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Help desk","report":"The new downtown satellite office must reach campus servers at all times, with no VPN software installed on staff PCs.","question":"Which VPN type fits?","options":["Site-to-site VPN","Client-to-site VPN","Clientless VPN","Split tunnel client VPN"],"answer":0,"why":"A site-to-site VPN connects the two networks through gateways, so user PCs need no software. like web traffic with TLS or a VPN tunnel. Encrypt data at rest, like a laptop drive or a database, so a stolen disk is useless. Certificates prove who a server is. In a PKI, a trusted certificate authority (CA) signs them. A self-signed certificate signs itself, so devices do not trust it by default and browsers show a warning.","hook":"Moving or sitting, encrypt it. A CA vouches; self-signed vouches for itself."},{"title":"Proving who you are","body":"Authentication proves identity. MFA uses two or more factor types: something you know, have, or are. SSO lets one login reach many apps, and SAML carries that login between an identity provider and an app.","hook":"MFA needs different types, not just more of the same."},{"title":"RADIUS versus TACACS+","body":"Both are AAA servers: authentication, authorization, and accounting. It obfuscates the whole packet body and separates all three A's. That makes it a favorite for controlling admin logins and commands on routers and switches.","hook":"TACACS+ talks TCP and guards the whole body. RADIUS hides only the password."},{"title":"Authorization and physical security","body":"Authorization decides what a user may do after login. Least privilege gives only the access a job needs. Physical security matters too. Cameras deter and record. Locks and badge readers keep people out of closets and data centers.","hook":"Least privilege: just enough, nothing extra."},{"title":"Risk words and decoys","body":"A vulnerability is a weakness, like an unpatched server. The CIA triad is confidentiality, integrity, and availability. A honeypot is a decoy system that lures attackers. A honeynet is a network of honeypots.","hook":"Weakness, danger, attack tool, odds times damage."},{"title":"Compliance and segmentation","body":"PCI DSS protects payment card data. A distributed DoS (DDoS) comes from many sources at once, often a botnet of infected devices. That makes blocking one address useless. The impact is on availability. Defenses include rate limiting, upstream filtering by the ISP, and cloud scrubbing or CDN services that absorb the flood.","hook":"One source is DoS. Many sources is DDoS. Both hit availability."},{"title":"How VLAN hopping works","body":"VLAN hopping lets traffic reach a VLAN it should not. In switch spoofing, a device pretends to be a switch and negotiates a trunk. In double tagging, a frame carries two 802.1Q tags. The first matches the native VLAN and is stripped, so the next switch reads the second tag. Defenses: set user ports to access mode, turn off trunk negotiation, and use an unused native VLAN.","hook":"Spoof a switch or stack two tags. Lock ports to access mode."},{"title":"MAC flooding and ARP attacks","body":"MAC flooding sends frames from thousands of fake MAC addresses. The switch MAC table fills, so it floods unknown frames out every port in the VLAN, like a hub. Port security limits MACs per port. ARP spoofing sends fake ARP replies, such as claiming the gateway's IP. ARP poisoning is the result: hosts cache the wrong MAC and send traffic to the attacker. Dynamic ARP Inspection blocks this.","hook":"Full table, switch acts like a hub. Lying ARP, traffic takes a detour."},{"title":"DNS poisoning and spoofing","body":"DNS poisoning plants false records, often in a resolver's cache or a host file. Users type the right name but land at the wrong IP address, such as a fake login page. DNS spoofing is forging DNS answers or posing as a DNS server. Signs include a correct URL that loads an unexpected site. DNSSEC lets resolvers check signatures and reject forged answers. Patching resolvers also helps.","hook":"Right name, wrong address: suspect DNS."},{"title":"Rogue devices and on-path","body":"A rogue DHCP server hands out wrong addresses, gateways, or DNS servers. DHCP snooping blocks server replies on user ports. A rogue AP is an unapproved access point plugged into the network. An evil twin is a fake AP copying a real SSID to lure users. An on-path attack sits between two hosts to read or change traffic. Encryption, 802.1X, and wireless intrusion prevention help.","hook":"Rogue joins your network. Evil twin copies your network."},{"title":"Social engineering and malware","body":"Social engineering tricks people instead of machines. Phishing uses fake messages to steal logins or deliver malware. Dumpster diving digs through trash for useful papers. Shoulder surfing watches screens or keypads. Tailgating is following an authorized person through a secure door. Malware is harmful software. Ransomware encrypts files for payment. A worm spreads across the network on its own. Training, shredding, and badge rules help.","hook":"Look, dig, follow, or fool. People are the target."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Athletics","report":"The ticket sales site went down minutes after playoff sales opened. If a new device breaks the rule, the switch can drop its traffic or shut the port down. Sticky learning saves the first MACs seen. MAC filtering is an allow or deny list of MAC addresses, often on Wi-Fi. It is weak alone because MAC addresses are easy to spoof.","hook":"MAC filtering is a speed bump, not a wall."},{"title":"802.1X and NAC","body":"Network access control (NAC) decides who and what may connect. 802.1X is port-based NAC for wired and wireless networks. The supplicant is the client device. The authenticator is the switch or AP. The authentication server, usually RADIUS, checks credentials. Until login succeeds, the port blocks normal traffic. The client and switch talk EAP over LAN (EAPOL), and the switch relays to RADIUS.","hook":"Supplicant asks, authenticator relays, server decides."},{"title":"Managing encryption keys safely","body":"Encryption is only as safe as its keys. NIST SP 800-57 is the main guide.","hook":"Make, share, store, rotate, revoke, destroy."},{"title":"ACLs and web filtering","body":"An access control list (ACL) is an ordered set of permit and deny rules. URL filtering allows or blocks websites by address or category. Content filtering looks at what is inside the traffic, like keywords, file types, or malware.","hook":"Top down, first match wins, the rest are denied."},{"title":"Trusted zones and screened subnets","body":"Firewalls sort networks into zones. It holds public-facing servers like a web or mail server. Internet users can reach those servers, but strict rules stop them from reaching the trusted inside network.","hook":"Public servers live in the screened subnet, not inside."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Data center","report":"An audit found a new core switch still accepts the vendor's factory admin login. Identify the problem. 2. Establish a theory of probable cause. 3. Test the theory to determine the cause. 4. Establish a plan of action to resolve the problem and identify potential effects. 5. Implement the solution or escalate as necessary. 6. Verify full system functionality and implement preventive measures if applicable. 7. Document findings, actions, outcomes, and lessons learned throughout the process.","hook":"Identify, Establish, Test, Establish, Implement, Verify, Document: In Every Town, Every Insect Visits Dinner."},{"title":"Step 1: Identify the problem","body":"Before you touch anything, learn what is really wrong. Gather information. Question users about what they see. Identify symptoms, such as error messages or lights. Determine if anything has changed, like an update or a moved cable. Duplicate the problem, if possible. If one call holds several issues, approach multiple problems individually, one ticket each.","hook":"Ask: what changed? Most outages start with a change."},{"title":"Step 2: Establish a theory","body":"Now guess the most likely cause. Question the obvious first: is it plugged in, powered on, on the right Wi-Fi? Then consider multiple approaches. Top-to-bottom starts at the application layer of the OSI model. Bottom-to-top starts at the physical layer. Divide and conquer starts in the middle, such as a ping at Layer 3, and moves up or down from the result."},{"title":"Step 3: Test the theory","body":"Test your theory to determine the cause. Swap the patch cable, try another port, or ping the gateway. If the theory is confirmed, determine the next steps to resolve the problem. If it is not confirmed, establish a new theory or escalate to someone with more access or skill. Do not jump to a fix until a test supports the cause.","hook":"Confirmed: plan the fix. Not confirmed: new theory or escalate."},{"title":"Steps 4 and 5: Plan, act","body":"Step 4 is to establish a plan of action to resolve the problem and identify potential effects. Ask who loses service, for how long, and whether a change window is needed. Step 5 is to implement the solution or escalate as necessary. Escalate when the fix needs rights, a vendor, or a team you do not have."},{"title":"Steps 6 and 7: Verify, document","body":"Step 6 is to verify full system functionality and implement preventive measures if applicable. Multimode has a wider core, 50 or 62.5 microns, for shorter runs. The optic, the run, and the patch cords must all match the same fiber type. Mixing them causes heavy signal loss, errors, or no link at all.","hook":"Single mode: one narrow path, long distance."},{"title":"Copper: category and shielding","body":"The cable category must support the link speed. Cat 5e supports 1 Gbps. Cat 6a supports 10 Gbps to 100 meters. Cat 8 supports 25 or 40 Gbps, but only to 30 meters. Shielded twisted pair (STP) resists electrical noise better than unshielded (UTP), but the shield must be grounded correctly. Use STP near motors and heavy electrical gear."},{"title":"Signal degradation causes","body":"Three things weaken a signal. Crosstalk is a signal bleeding into a nearby pair, often from pairs untwisted too far at a jack. Interference, or EMI, comes from motors, power lines, and lights. Attenuation is signal loss over distance. Copper Ethernet allows 100 meters per channel. Past that, expect errors and dropped links.","hook":"Crosstalk is pairs, interference is outside noise, attenuation is distance."},{"title":"Termination and TX/RX swap","body":"Improper termination means a bad jack or plug: wrong pinout, loose wires, or too much untwisting. A cable tester shows the wire map fault. On fiber, each side's transmit (TX) must reach the other side's receive (RX). If TX/RX are transposed, the link stays down. Swap the two strands at one end. Most copper ports fix crossover with Auto-MDIX."},{"title":"Interface error counters","body":"Watch counters that keep increasing. CRC errors mean frames failed the checksum, usually from bad cabling, optics, or a NIC. Runts are frames smaller than the 64-byte Ethernet minimum. Giants are frames larger than the port's maximum size, often an MTU or jumbo frame mismatch. Drops are frames discarded, often because buffers filled. Clear counters, then recheck."},{"title":"Port status, PoE, transceivers","body":"Error disabled means the switch shut the port, for example a BPDU guard or port security violation. PoE: 802.3af gives 15.4 W, 802.3at 30 W, 802.3bt up to 90 W per port. Optics must match, with healthy receive power.","hook":"Err-disabled is the switch's choice. Without it, a loop causes a broadcast storm, flapping MAC tables, and a crawling network. STP elects one root bridge: the switch with the lowest bridge ID. The bridge ID is a priority, 32768 by default, plus the MAC address. Set a low priority on the core so an old switch never wins.","hook":"Lowest bridge ID wins root."},{"title":"STP port roles and states","body":"Each switch picks a root port, its best path to the root bridge. Each segment has one designated port that forwards. In Rapid STP, alternate and backup ports are blocked spares. Classic 802.1D states are blocking, listening, learning, forwarding, and disabled. Rapid STP uses only discarding, learning, and forwarding. Discarding replaces disabled, blocking, and listening."},{"title":"VLANs and ACLs","body":"If a port is in the wrong VLAN, the device lands in the wrong subnet. It may get the wrong DHCP address or none, and lose access to its resources. Check the port's VLAN on the switch. ACLs are read top to bottom, and the first match wins. Every ACL ends with an implicit deny, so traffic you did not permit is dropped."},{"title":"Route selection and defaults","body":"A router forwards using the most specific match, called the longest prefix match. A /24 beats a /16. Administrative distance only breaks ties between sources for the same prefix. On Cisco, connected is 0, static is 1, OSPF is 110, and RIP is 120. The default route, 0.0.0.0/0, catches anything with no better match. Without it, internet traffic fails.","hook":"Most specific prefix first. Distance only breaks ties."},{"title":"DHCP address pool exhaustion","body":"A DHCP scope has a limited number of addresses. When they are all leased, new clients get nothing. Windows clients then self-assign an APIPA address from 169.254.0.0/16 and can only talk locally. Older devices that already hold leases keep working. Fixes include a larger scope, shorter leases for busy guest networks, and clearing stale leases.","hook":"169.254 means DHCP did not answer."},{"title":"Bad IP settings","body":"Wrong default gateway: the host reaches local devices but nothing on other subnets. Duplicate IP address: two hosts share one address, so connections drop on and off and the OS may warn of a conflict. Wrong subnet mask: the host misjudges which addresses are local, so some nearby hosts or remote hosts fail. Compare settings to a working neighbor."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Data center","report":"A manager asks how spanning tree decides which switch becomes the root bridge.","question":"Which switch becomes the root bridge?","options":["The switch with the most ports","The switch with the lowest bridge ID","The switch with the highest priority value","The switch powered on first"],"answer":1,"why":"The lowest bridge ID wins, which is priority first, then MAC address. Throughput is how much data actually gets through. Throughput is always lower because of protocol overhead, errors, retransmissions, and shared use. When a user says the network is slow, measure throughput and compare it to the link's capacity.","hook":"Bandwidth is the pipe. Throughput is the water."},{"title":"Congestion and bottlenecks","body":"Congestion, or contention, happens when more traffic wants a link or medium than it can carry. Queues fill and packets drop. Wi-Fi clients also contend for airtime on one channel. A bottleneck is the slowest point in the path, and it limits the whole path. One 1 Gbps uplink shared by many busy 1 Gbps clients is a common bottleneck."},{"title":"Latency, jitter, packet loss","body":"Latency is the delay for a packet to cross the network. Jitter is the variation in that delay from packet to packet. Packet loss is packets that never arrive. File downloads mostly survive these, because TCP retransmits. Voice and video suffer most: jitter and loss cause choppy audio and frozen video. QoS can prioritize real-time traffic.","hook":"Jitter is uneven delay, not just high delay."},{"title":"Wireless interference and overlap","body":"In the US 2.4 GHz band, only channels 1, 6, and 11 do not overlap. APs on channels like 1 and 3 overlap and interfere. Non-Wi-Fi devices interfere too. Microwave ovens and Bluetooth both use 2.4 GHz. The 5 GHz band has many more non-overlapping channels. Use a Wi-Fi analyzer to see channel use.","hook":"2.4 GHz: 1, 6, 11."},{"title":"Signal and coverage","body":"Wi-Fi signal degrades with distance and through walls, floors, metal, and water. Weak signal means lower data rates, retries, and drops. Insufficient wireless coverage leaves dead zones where clients cannot connect. A site survey maps signal strength before and after a change. Fixes include moving APs, adding APs, or adjusting power and antennas."},{"title":"Disassociation and roaming","body":"Client disassociation is a device dropping off its AP. Causes include weak signal, interference, or forged deauthentication frames from an attacker. Roaming is moving between APs without losing the session. For it to work, APs need the same SSID and matching security settings. Too much AP power can create sticky clients that cling to a distant AP."}],"tickets":[{"place":"Help desk","report":"A manager reviewing VoIP complaints asks you to define jitter in plain terms.","question":"What is jitter?","options":["Total bandwidth of a link","Variation in packet delay over time","Percentage of packets that never arrive","Time to resolve a hostname"],"answer":1,"why":"Jitter is the variation in delay between packets. Windows sends 4 by default, and ping -t runs until stopped. Linux pings until you press Ctrl+C, or use -c to set a count. Windows tracert sends ICMP echo requests. Linux traceroute uses UDP."},{"title":"DNS and IP config tools","body":"nslookup queries DNS on Windows and Linux. dig is the detailed DNS tool on Linux and macOS, as in dig example.edu MX. On Linux, the ip command (ip addr, ip route) replaces the older ifconfig and route."},{"title":"netstat, arp, tcpdump, analyzers","body":"netstat shows connections and listening ports. A speed tester measures real throughput."},{"title":"Hardware troubleshooting tools","body":"A toner and probe traces one cable through walls or a patch panel. const MON = ["Jan","Feb","Mar","Apr","May","Jun","Jul","Aug","Sep","Oct","Nov","Dec"];
function parseDay(str){ const [y,m,d] = str.split("-").map(Number); return new Date(y, m-1, d); }
function fmtDay(dt){ return MON[dt.getMonth()] + " " + dt.getDate(); }
function mondayOf(dt){ const x = new Date(dt.getFullYear(), dt.getMonth(), dt.getDate()); x.setDate(x.getDate() - ((x.getDay() + 6) % 7)); return x; }
function planWeeks(){
  const st = parseDay(S.planStart || todayStr(mondayOf(new Date())));

}

function h(tag, attrs, ...kids){
  const el = document.createElementNS(tag.startsWith("svg:") ? "http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" : "http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml", tag.replace("svg:",""));
  if (attrs) for (const [k,v] of Object.entries(attrs)){
    if (v == null || v === false) continue;
    if (k === "on") for (const [ev,fn] of Object.entries(v)) el.addEventListener(ev, fn);
    else if (k === "text") el.textContent = v;
    else el.setAttribute(k, v === true ? "" : v);
  }
  for (const k of kids.flat()) if (k != null && k !== false) el.append(k.nodeType ? k : document.createTextNode(String(k)));
  return el;
}
const put = (el, ...kids) => el.replaceChildren(...kids.flat(2).filter(k => k != null && k !== false));
const todayStr = (d=new Date()) => { const z = new Date(d.getTime() - d.getTimezoneOffset()*60000); return z.toISOString().slice(0,10); };
function shuffle(a){ a = a.slice(); for (let i=a.length-1;i>0;i--){ const j=Math.floor(Math.random()*(i+1)); [a[i],a[j]]=[a[j],a[i]]; } return a; }
function toast(msg){ const t=$("#toast"); t.textContent=msg; t.hidden=false; clearTimeout(toast.t); toast.t=setTimeout(()=>t.hidden=true, 2600); }

/* ---------- persistence: this browser only (localStorage), plus export and import ---------- */
const LS = "campus-restore-v1";
let storageOK = true;
function loadLocal(){ try { const r = localStorage.getItem(LS); return r ? JSON.parse(r) : null; } catch { storageOK = false; return null; } }
function save(){
  S.updatedAt = Date.now();
  try { localStorage.setItem(LS, JSON.stringify(S)); storageOK = true; } catch { storageOK = false; }
  setSync(storageOK ? "Saved in this browser" : "Not saving: export your progress");
}
function setSync(t){ $("#st-sync").textContent = t; }
function exportProgress(){
  const blob = new Blob([JSON.stringify(S, null, 1)], {type:"application/json"});
  const a = h("a", {href: URL.createObjectURL(blob), download: "campus-restore-progress-" + todayStr() + ".json"});
  document.body.append(a); a.click(); setTimeout(() => { URL.revokeObjectURL(a.href); a.remove(); }, 500);
  toast("Progress file downloaded");
}
function importProgress(file){
  const r = new FileReader();
  r.onload = () => {
    try {
      const j = JSON.parse(r.result);
      if (!j || typeof j !== "object" || typeof j.xp !== "number" || typeof j.obj !== "object") throw 0;
      S = Object.assign(blank(), j); migrateHist(); save(); renderAll(); toast("Progress loaded");
    } catch { toast("That file isn't a Campus Restore progress file"); }
  };
  r.readAsText(file);
}
/* ---------- progress logic ---------- */
const O = id => (S.obj[id] ||= {learned:false, best:0, stars:0, shifts:0, reviews:0, last:0, missed:[]});
function status(id){
  const o = S.obj[id];
  if (!o || !o.learned) return "down";
  if (!o.stars) return "learn";
  const due = o.last + REVIEW_DAYS[Math.min(o.reviews, REVIEW_DAYS.length-1)] * DAY;
  return Date.now() > due ? "flick" : "up";
}
const STATUS_TXT = {down:"Down", learn:"Studied", up:"Up", flick:"Review due"};

function addXP(n){
  const before = Math.floor(S.xp / XP_PER_LEVEL);
  S.xp += n;
  if (Math.floor(S.xp / XP_PER_LEVEL) > before) setTimeout(() => toast("Promoted: " + levelName()), 400);
}
function levelName(){ const l = Math.floor(S.xp / XP_PER_LEVEL); return "Level " + (l+1) + " · " + TITLES[Math.min(l, TITLES.length-1)]; }
function bumpStreak(){
  const t = todayStr(), y = todayStr(new Date(Date.now() - DAY));
  if (S.streak.last === t) return;
  S.streak.count = S.streak.last === y ? S.streak.count + 1 : 1;
  S.streak.last = t;
}
function streakNow(){ const t=todayStr(), y=todayStr(new Date(Date.now()-DAY)); return (S.streak.last===t||S.streak.last===y) ? S.streak.count : 0; }
function allObjIds(){ return DOMAINS.flatMap(d => (D[d.id]?.objectives||[]).map(o => o.id)); }
function currentWeek(){
  const t = todayStr();
  let cur = null;
  for (const p of planWeeks()) if (t >= p.from) cur = p;
  return cur;
}

function renderMap(){
  const svg = $("#map"); svg.replaceChildren();
  const hub = DOMAINS[4];
  /* cables from hub to each building, one dot per objective */
  DOMAINS.slice(0,4).forEach(d => {
    const objs = D[d.id]?.objectives || [];
    const sts = objs.map(o => status(o.id));
    const allUp = sts.length && sts.every(s => s === "up" || s === "flick");
    svg.append(h("svg:line", {x1:hub.x, y1:hub.y, x2:d.x, y2:d.y, stroke: allUp ? "var(--s-green)" : "var(--screen-3)", "stroke-width": allUp ? 3 : 2.5, "stroke-dasharray": allUp ? null : "5 4"}));
    objs.forEach((o, i) => {
      const t = (i + 1) / (objs.length + 1);
      const cx = hub.x + (d.x - hub.x) * t, cy = hub.y + (d.y - hub.y) * t;
      const st = sts[i];
      svg.append(h("svg:circle", {cx, cy, r:5.5, fill:STATUS_COL[st], stroke:"var(--screen)", "stroke-width":1.5, class: st==="flick" ? "flick" : null}, h("svg:title", {text: o.id + " " + SHORT[o.id] + ": " + STATUS_TXT[st]})));
    });
  });
  /* hub ring for domain 5 objectives */
  const hobjs = D[hub.id]?.objectives || [];
  hobjs.forEach((o, i) => {
    const a = -Math.PI/2 + i * 2*Math.PI / hobjs.length;
    const st = status(o.id);
    svg.append(h("svg:circle", {cx: hub.x + Math.cos(a)*46, cy: hub.y + Math.sin(a)*40, r:5.5, fill:STATUS_COL[st], stroke:"var(--screen)", "stroke-width":1.5, class: st==="flick" ? "flick" : null}, h("svg:title", {text: o.id + " " + SHORT[o.id] + ": " + STATUS_TXT[st]})));
  });
  DOMAINS.forEach(d => {
    const objs = D[d.id]?.objectives || [];
    const up = objs.filter(o => ["up","flick"].includes(status(o.id))).length;
    const pct = objs.length ? up / objs.length : 0;
    const fill = pct === 1 ? "var(--green)" : pct > 0 ? "var(--orange)" : "var(--red)";
    const w = 96, hh = 50;
    const g = h("svg:g", {class: "bld" + (d.id === selDomain ? " sel" : ""), tabindex:0, role:"button", "aria-label": d.place + ", domain " + d.id + " " + d.name + ", " + up + " of " + objs.length + " links up",
      on:{click:() => selectDomain(d.id), keydown:e => { if (e.key==="Enter"||e.key===" "){ e.preventDefault(); selectDomain(d.id);} }}});
    g.append(
      h("svg:rect", {class:"box", x:d.x-w/2, y:d.y-hh/2, width:w, height:hh, rx:9, fill:"var(--screen-2)", stroke:fill, "stroke-width":2}),
      h("svg:rect", {x:d.x-w/2+8, y:d.y+hh/2-11, width:(w-16), height:4, rx:2, fill:"var(--screen-3)"}),
      h("svg:rect", {x:d.x-w/2+8, y:d.y+hh/2-11, width:Math.max(0,(w-16)*pct), height:4, rx:2, fill:fill}),
      h("svg:text", {x:d.x, y:d.y-8, "text-anchor":"middle", "font-size":10.5, "font-weight":600, fill:"var(--screen-ink)", text:d.place}),
      h("svg:text", {x:d.x, y:d.y+5, "text-anchor":"middle", "font-size":8.5, fill:"var(--screen-muted)", text:d.id + " · " + d.weight + "% · " + up + "/" + objs.length})
    );
    svg.append(g);
  });
  arcadeMapMarker(svg);
}
